    Indeed, by \eqref{eq:ass_noise2} and properties of Fourier transform, it holds $R\in C^2_x$ with $\| R\|_{C^2_x}\lesssim c_2'$, therefore \autoref{ex:ass_noise} applies.
<!-- end rmk-10 -->
<!-- prop-4: body_md -->
\label{thm_pathwise_uniq_order_0}
Let $W$ satisfy \autoref{ass:noise2} and let $(\alpha,p,r,\mathscr{R})$ satisfy \autoref{ass:strong_regularization} with $\mathscr{R}=\mathscr{R}_{CZ}$.
Let $\theta_0\in L^2_x\cap L^p_x$ and $f\in L^1_T (L^2_x\cap L^{p}_x)$ be fixed.
Then, in the strictly subcritical case $\alpha<1/2-1/p$, pathwise uniqueness in $L^\infty_{\omega,T}(L^2_x\cap L^p_x)$ holds for weak solutions of \eqref{eq:nonlinear_transport}.
Similarly, in the critical case $\alpha=1/2-1/p$, pathwise uniqueness in $L^\infty_{\omega,T}(L^{2}_x\cap L^p_x)\cap \mathcal{U}_p$ holds for weak solutions of \eqref{eq:nonlinear_transport}, with $\mathcal{U}_p$ being defined by \eqref{eq:defn_Up}.
<!-- end prop-4 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
The argument is inspired by the previous works \cite{CoMa23,GaGrMa24,crippa2025zero}, based on looking at the evolution of appropriate negative Sobolev norms.
Let us first present the argument in the subcritical regime.

Let $\theta^1,\theta^2 \in L^\infty_{\omega,T}(L^r_x\cap L^p_x)$ be two weak solutions on the same tuple $\left(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_t,\mathbb{P},W\right)$, and denote $\rho_t:={\theta}^1_t-{\theta}^2_t$ their difference and let $\hat{\rho}_t$ be its Fourier transform.  
Arguing analogously to \cite[Section A.1]{crippa2025zero}, calling $a_t(\xi):=\mathbb{E}\left[|\hat{\rho}_t(\xi)|^2\right]$ and $e_{\xi}:=(2\pi)^{-1} e^{-i\xi\cdot x}$, for any $n\geqslant 1$ it holds that
\begin{align}\label{Ito_uniqueness}
    \int_{\mathbb{R}^2}  a_t(\xi)\langle \xi\rangle^{2(\alpha-1)}\varphi_n(\xi) \,\mathrm{d}\xi
    &=
    \int_0^t \int_{\mathbb{R}^2}a_s(\xi) F^n_{1-\alpha}(\xi) \,\mathrm{d}\xi \,\mathrm{d} s\notag\notag\\ 
    & -2\int_0^t\int_{\mathbb{R}^2}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathfrak{Re} \left(\overline{\hat{\rho}_s(\xi)} \langle u^1_s \cdot \nabla \theta^1_s-u^2_s \cdot \nabla \theta^2_s , e_\xi \rangle \right)\right] \langle \xi\rangle^{2(\alpha-1)}\varphi_n(\xi) \,\mathrm{d}\xi \,\mathrm{d} s,
\end{align}
where $\varphi_n$ are suitable frequency cut-off functions and the ``flux'' functions $F^n_{1-\alpha}$ are defined in \autoref{Appendix:approx_kernels}. 
By dominated convergence, we have 
\begin{align}\label{conve_1_uniq}
     \lim_{n\to\infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} a_t(\xi)\langle \xi\rangle^{2(\alpha-1)}\varphi_n(\xi) \,\mathrm{d} \xi = \mathbb{E}\left[\|\rho_t\|_{H^{\alpha-1}_x}^2\right].
\end{align}
By assumption $\rho\in L^\infty_{\omega,T} L^2_x$, thus $a_s\in L^\infty_{T}L^1_{\xi}$; in view of estimate \eqref{eq:pointwise_bound_approx} from \autoref{lem:flux_functions} (with $s=1-\alpha$), $F^n_{1-\alpha}$ are uniformly bounded. Therefore by dominated convergence and \eqref{eq:good_bound}, we find
\begin{align}\label{conve_2_uniq}
     \lim_{n\to\infty} \int_0^t \int_{\mathbb{R}^2}a_s(\xi) F^n_{1-\alpha}(\xi) \,\mathrm{d}\xi \,\mathrm{d} s
     &= \int_0^t \int_{\mathbb{R}^2}a_s(\xi) F_{1-\alpha}(\xi) \,\mathrm{d}\xi \,\mathrm{d} s\notag\\ 
     &\leqslant -K_1\int_0^t \mathbb{E}\left[\|\rho_s\|_{L^2_x}^2 \right] \,\mathrm{d} s+K_2\int_0^t\mathbb{E}\left[ \|\rho_s\|_{H^{\alpha-1}_x}^2\right] \,\mathrm{d} s
\end{align}
for some $K_1,K_2>0.$ We are left to estimate the nonlinear term. In the case $\alpha<1/2-1/p$, calling $\gamma:=1-2\alpha-2/p> 0$, by H\"older's inequality and the Sobolev embedding 
\begin{align*}
L^{\frac{2p}{p+2}}_x\hookrightarrow H^{-\frac{2}{p}}_x=H^{2\alpha-1+\gamma}_x,
\end{align*}
it holds 
\begin{align}\label{conve_3_uniq}
\bigg|\int_0^t\int_{\mathbb{R}^2}\mathbb{E}& \left[\mathfrak{Re} \left(\overline{\hat{\rho}_s(\xi)} \langle u^1_s \cdot \nabla \theta^1_s-u^2_s \cdot \nabla \theta^2_s , e_\xi \rangle \right)\right] \langle \xi\rangle^{2(\alpha-1)}\varphi_n(\xi) \,\mathrm{d}\xi \,\mathrm{d} s\bigg|\notag\\
& \leqslant \int_0^t\int_{\mathbb{R}^2}\mathbb{E}\left[|\hat{\rho}_s(\xi)| |\langle u^1_s \rho_s+(\mathscr{R}_{CZ}\rho_s) \theta^2_s, e_\xi \rangle|\right] |\xi|\langle \xi\rangle^{2(\alpha-1)}\varphi_n(\xi) \,\mathrm{d}\xi \,\mathrm{d} s\notag\\
& \leqslant \int_0^t \mathbb{E} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} |\hat{\rho}_s(\xi)|  \big(|\widehat{u^1_s \rho_s}(\xi)|+|\widehat{(\mathscr{R}_{CZ}\rho_s) \theta^2_s}(\xi)| \big)\langle \xi\rangle^{2\alpha-1} \, \,\mathrm{d}\xi \,\mathrm{d} s \notag\\
& \lesssim \int_0^t \mathbb{E}\left[\|\rho_s\|_{H^{-\gamma}_x}^2\right]^{1/2} \mathbb{E}\left[\|u^1_s \rho_s\|_{H^{2\alpha-1+\gamma}_x}^2+\|\mathscr{R}_{CZ}\rho_s \theta^2_s\|_{H^{2\alpha-1+\gamma}_x}^2\right]^{1/2} \,\mathrm{d} s\notag\\
& \lesssim 
\int_0^t \mathbb{E}\left[\|\rho_s\|_{H^{-\gamma}_x}^2\right]^{1/2} 
\mathbb{E}\left[\|u^1_s \rho_s\|_{L^{\frac{2p}{p+2}}_x}^2+\|\mathscr{R}_{CZ}\rho_s \theta^2_s\|_{L^{\frac{2p}{p+2}}_x}^2\right]^{1/2} \,\mathrm{d} s\notag\\
& \lesssim 
\big( \| \theta^1\|_{L^\infty_{\omega,T} L^p_x} + \| \theta^2\|_{L^\infty_{\omega,T} L^p_x}\big)\int_0^t \mathbb{E}\left[\|\rho_s\|_{H^{-\gamma}_x}^2\right]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\rho_s\|_{L^2_x}^2\right]^{1/2} \,\mathrm{d} s,
\end{align}
uniformly in $n$. Letting $n\rightarrow +\infty$ in \eqref{Ito_uniqueness}, combining \eqref{conve_1_uniq}, \eqref{conve_2_uniq}, \eqref{conve_3_uniq} and Young's inequality, we obtain
\begin{align*}
\mathbb{E}\left[\|\rho_t\|_{H^{\alpha-1}_x}^2\right]+\frac{K_1}{2}\int_0^t \mathbb{E}\left[\|\rho_s\|_{L^2_x}^2\right] \,\mathrm{d} s \lesssim \int_0^t \mathbb{E}\left[\|\rho_s\|_{H^{\alpha-1}_x}^2\right] \,\mathrm{d} s.
\end{align*}
The latter implies the claim by Gr\"onwall's inequality.

In the critical case $\alpha=1/2-1/p$, it holds $\gamma=0$, so \eqref{conve_3_uniq} together with Young's inequality does not allow to conclude anymore.
In this case, we follow the argument from \cite{bagnara2025regularization}. 

For $\varepsilon>0$ to be fixed later, consider a decomposition of $\theta^1,\theta^2$ such that
\begin{align*}
    \|\theta^{1,<}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega,T}L^p_x}\leqslant \varepsilon,\quad \|\theta^{2,<}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega,T}L^p_x}\leqslant \varepsilon,\quad  \|\theta^{1,>}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega,T}(L^p_x\cap L^{q}_x)}\leqslant M_{1,\varepsilon},\quad \|\theta^{2,>}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega,T}(L^p_x\cap L^{q}_x)}\leqslant M_{2,\varepsilon}
\end{align*}
for some $q\in (p,+\infty).$ Then arguing as above, it holds
\begin{align}\label{conve_3_uniq_2}
\int_0^t\int_{\mathbb{R}^2}\mathbb{E} & \left[\mathfrak{Re} \left(\overline{\hat{\rho}_s(\xi)} \langle u^1_s \cdot \nabla \theta^1_s-u^2_s \cdot \nabla \theta^2_s , e_\xi \rangle \right)\right] \langle \xi\rangle^{2(\alpha-1)}\varphi_n(\xi) \,\mathrm{d}\xi \,\mathrm{d} s\notag\\
& \leqslant C_p \int_0^t \mathbb{E}\left[\|\rho_s\|_{L^{2}_x}^2\right]^{1/2} \mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathscr{R}_{CZ}\left(\theta^{1,<}_s+\theta^{1,>}_s\right) \rho_s\|_{L^{\frac{2p}{p+2}}_x}^2
+
\|\mathscr{R}_{CZ}\rho_s \left(\theta^{2,<}_s+\theta^{2,>}_s\right)\|_{L^{\frac{2p}{p+2}}_x}^2\right]^{1/2} \,\mathrm{d} s\notag\\
& \leqslant \varepsilon C_p\int_0^t \mathbb{E}\left[\|\rho_s\|_{L^{2}_x}^2\right]\,\mathrm{d} s+C_p(M_{1,\varepsilon}+M_{2,\varepsilon})\int_0^t\mathbb{E}\left[\|\rho_s\|_{L^{2}_x}^2\right]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\rho_s\|_{H^{-\gamma_{\varepsilon}}_x}^2\right]^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d} s,
\end{align}
for some $\gamma_{\varepsilon}\in (0,1-\alpha)$ and some constant $C_p>0$ possibly changing line by line, but independent of the choice of $\varepsilon.$ Thus, we can first fix $\varepsilon$ small enough such that $\varepsilon C_p\leqslant K_1/4$, then \eqref{conve_3_uniq_2} allows to conclude by Young's inequality as in the case $\alpha<1/2-1/p$.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- rmk-11: body_md -->
\label{rmk:pathwise_uniqueness}
    In the strictly subcritical regime $\alpha<1/2 - 1/p$, the previous \autoref{thm_pathwise_uniq_order_0} provides pathwise uniqueness in the class $\theta\in L^\infty_{\omega,T}(L^r_x \cap L^p_x)$, without any additional $\tilde L^2_{\omega,T} \tilde B^\beta_{2,\infty}$-regularity of solutions required. In particular, this class is strictly larger than the one to which solutions obtained by vanishing viscosity scheme (\cref{thm:regularization}) belong. 
    A similar remark applies in the critical regime $\alpha = 1/2-1/p$, up to including the space $\mathcal{U}_p$.
<!-- end rmk-11 -->
<!-- rmk-12: body_md -->
As shown in \cite[Theorem 1.1]{castro2025unstable}, non-uniqueness of solutions in $L^\infty_{T}(L^r_x \cap L^p_x)$ holds for the deterministic SQG equation with forcing.
The presence of rough transport noise genuinely restores wellposedness for this SPDE.
Similar considerations apply for IPM, where in the absence of noise uniqueness fails for $L^\infty_{T,x}$-solutions, as shown by convex integration techniques \cite{cordoba2011lack}.
<!-- end rmk-12 -->
<!-- prop-5: body_md -->
\label{thm_pathwise_uniq_euler}
Let $W$ satisfy \autoref{ass:noise2} and let $(\alpha,p,r,\mathscr{R})$ satisfy \autoref{ass:strong_regularization} with $\mathscr{R}=\mathscr{R}_{BS}$.
Then for any $\theta_0\in L^r_x\cap L^p_x$ and deterministic forcing $f\in L^1_T (L^r_x\cap L^{p}_x)$, pathwise uniqueness in $L^\infty_{\omega,T}(L^r_x\cap L^p_x)$ holds for weak solutions of \eqref{eq:nonlinear_transport}.
<!-- end prop-5 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Let $\theta^1,\theta^2$ be two solutions belonging to $L^\infty_{\omega,T}(L^r_x\cap L^p_x)$ and defined on the same probability space. Thanks to \autoref{prop:convergence_law}, we have weak existence of another weak solution in $L^\infty_{\omega,T}(L^r_x\cap L^p_x)\cap \mathcal{U}_p$; therefore, using the Yamada--Watanabe argument from \cite[Lemma 4.2]{bagnara2025regularization}, without loss of generality we may assume $\theta^2\in L^\infty_{\omega,T}(L^r_x\cap L^p_x)\cap \mathcal{U}_p$ whenever needed in the proof of pathwise uniqueness.


Set $u^i := \mathscr{R}_{BS} \theta^i$, $\rho:=\theta^1-\theta^2$, $u:=u^1-u^2$; since the deterministic forcing $f$ cancels out in the equation for $\rho$, we may ignore it.
The argument is similar to that of \cite[Theorem 1.1]{BaGa25}, based on a Gr\"onwall-type argument for the evolution of $\mathbb{E}[\| \rho_t \|^2_{\dot{H}^{-1}_x}]$, exploiting the specific cancellations from the Euler nonlinearity.
The key point, compared to \cite{BaGa25}, is that even though $\theta^i$ do not need to belong to $L^2_{\omega,T}\dot{H}^{-1}_x$, their difference $\rho$ still does. Let us preliminarily show this fact.

For this purpose, fix an auxiliary parameter $\ell\in(r,2)$ and set
\begin{align*}
q:=\frac{2\ell}{2-\ell}>\frac{2r}{2-r} \geqslant 2,
\qquad
\gamma:=\frac{2}{q}\in(0,1).
\end{align*}
Recall that $\rho\in L^\infty_{\omega,t}L^2_x$ since $r<2\leqslant p$ by assumption.
Moreover, by \eqref{eq:properties_BS_integrability} and our choice of the parameter $q$ we have for each $i\in\{1,2\}$
\begin{equation}\label{bound_velocity}
\|u^i\|_{L^\infty_{\omega,t}L^q_x}
\lesssim
\|\theta^i\|_{L^\infty_{\omega,t}(L^r_x\cap L^p_x)}.
\end{equation}
As in
\cite[Section~2.3]{BaGa25}, for $\delta\in(0,1)$, define the following approximation of the Green function $G$ associated to $(-\Delta)^{-1}$:
\begin{align*}
G^\delta(x):=\int_\delta^{1/\delta}p_s(x)\,\mathrm{d} s,
\qquad
m_\delta(\xi):=\int_\delta^{1/\delta}
e^{-s|\xi|^2}\,\mathrm{d} s,
\end{align*}
where $p_s$ denotes the heat kernel on $\mathbb R^2$,
so that $m_\delta$ is the Fourier multiplier of convolution
with $G^\delta$. 
Set
\begin{align*}
e^\delta_t:=
\langle G^\delta*\rho_t,\rho_t\rangle,
\qquad
E^\delta_t:=\mathbb E[e^\delta_t].
\end{align*}
Since $0\leqslant |\xi|^2m_\delta(\xi)\leqslant1$, Plancherel's identity
gives, for every $z\in L^2_x$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:estimate_reg}
\|\nabla G^\delta*z\|_{L^2_x}^2
\leqslant \langle G^\delta*z,z\rangle,
\qquad
\|D^2G^\delta*z\|_{L^2_x}
\leqslant \|z\|_{L^2_x}.
\end{equation}
Combining these estimates with the
Gagliardo--Nirenberg inequality, we obtain
\begin{align}\label{eq:gagliardo_niremberg}
\|\nabla G^\delta*\rho_s\|_{L^{2q/(q-2)}_x}
\lesssim_q
\|\nabla G^\delta*\rho_s\|_{L^2_x}^{1-\gamma}
\|D^2G^\delta*\rho_s\|_{L^2_x}^{\gamma}\leqslant
(e^\delta_s)^{(1-\gamma)/2}
\|\rho_s\|_{L^2_x}^{\gamma}.
\end{align}

Arguing as in \cite[Section~4]{BaGa25}, we apply Itô's formula to $e_\delta(t)$ and take expectations.
In this step we observe that $\rho$ has zero initial condition and forcing (as they were the same for $\theta^1$ and $\theta^2$), and
the contribution coming from the term with $C(0) : D^2 \rho$ is non positive;
overall, we get
\begin{align}\label{eq:regularized_ito_euler}
E^\delta_t
&\lesssim
\int_0^t
\mathbb E\bigl[
\langle\nabla G^\delta*\rho_s,J_s\rangle
\bigr]\,\mathrm{d} s
 +
\sum_{k\in\mathbb N}\int_0^t
\mathbb E\bigl[
\langle
G^\delta*\operatorname{div}(\sigma_k\rho_s),
\operatorname{div}(\sigma_k\rho_s)
\rangle
\bigr]\,\mathrm{d} s
\end{align}
where $J:=u^1\theta^1-u^2\theta^2$.
Let us control the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:regularized_ito_euler} to apply Gr\"onwall. First, by Hölder's inequality,  \eqref{eq:gagliardo_niremberg}, \eqref{bound_velocity}, and the uniform $L^2_x$ bounds on the
solutions, we have
\begin{align*}
|\langle\nabla G^\delta*\rho_s,J_s\rangle|
&\leqslant
\|\nabla G^\delta*\rho_s\|_{L^{2q/(q-2)}_x}
\sum_{i=1}^2
\|u^i_s\|_{L^q_x}\|\theta^i_s\|_{L^2_x}\\
&\lesssim
e_\delta(s)^{(1-\gamma)/2}
\|\rho_s\|_{L^2_x}^{\gamma}
\sum_{i=1}^2
\|u^i_s\|_{L^q_x}\|\theta^i_s\|_{L^2_x}
\lesssim 
1+e_\delta(s),
\end{align*}
where all implicit constants are independent of $\delta$.
Secondly, the bound $|\xi|^2m_\delta(\xi)\leqslant1$ gives
\begin{align*}
\sum_{k\in\mathbb N}
\langle
G^\delta*\operatorname{div}(\sigma_k\rho_s),
\operatorname{div}(\sigma_k\rho_s)
\rangle\leqslant
\sum_{k\in\mathbb N}\|\sigma_k\rho_s\|_{L^2_x}^2
=
\operatorname{Tr}C(0)\,\|\rho_s\|_{L^2_x}^2
\lesssim 1.
\end{align*}
Combining these estimates with
\eqref{eq:regularized_ito_euler}, we obtain
\begin{align*}
E^\delta_t
\lesssim \int_0^t (1+E^\delta_s)\,\mathrm{d} s,
\end{align*}
and Grönwall's lemma yields $\sup_{\delta\in(0,1), t\in[0,T]}
E^\delta_t < \infty$.
Since $m_\delta(\xi)\uparrow |\xi|^{-2}$ as $\delta\rightarrow 0$
for every $k\neq0$, monotone convergence then implies the desired $\rho \in L^\infty_T L^2_\omega\dot H^{-1}_x$;
as a consequence $u\in L^\infty_T L^2_\omega  H^1_x$. 

With this additional information on $\rho$, we can now follow the lines of the proof of \cite[Theorem 1.1]{BaGa25} to get the equivalent of their Equation (4.4), and then conclude by Gr\"onwall's lemma; let us only briefly sketch one possible argument.
Notice that, due to the structure of the Euler nonlinearity, we have $\rho_t = \int_0^ t f_s \mathrm{d} s + \nabla\cdot M_t$, where
\begin{align*}
    f_t:= -\nabla^\perp\cdot [\nabla\cdot (u\otimes u^1 + u^2\otimes u)],\qquad 
    M_t := \int_0^t \rho_s\, \mathrm{d} W_s.
\end{align*}
Using that $u\in L^2_{\omega,T} H^1_x$ by the above and $u^1,u^2\in L^\infty_{\omega,T} L^q_x$ with $q=2r/(2-r)$ by \eqref{eq:properties_BS_integrability}, it's easy to see that $u\otimes u^1 + u^2\otimes u\in L^2_{\omega,T,x}$, thus $f\in L^2_{\omega,T} \dot H^{-2}_x$. Similarly, $\nabla\cdot M$ is a continuous $\dot H^{-1}_x$-valued martingale by \cite[Lemma 2.2]{DrGaPa25}.
With these facts in mind, as well as $\rho\in L^\infty_{\omega,t} L^2_x$, one can rigorously pass to the limit as $\delta\to 0^+$ in the It\^o formula for $E^\delta$ and take expectation to find
\begin{align*}
    \mathbb{E}[\| \rho_t\|_{\dot H^{-1}_x}^2] = -2\int_0^t \mathbb{E}[ \langle u_s, (u_s\cdot\nabla) u^2_s) \rangle] + \mathbb{E}\left[ \int_0^t \langle \mathrm{Tr} [QD^2G] \ast \rho_s ,\rho_s \rangle \, \mathrm{d} s \right]
\end{align*}
where we exploited the cancellation $\langle u_s, (u^1_s\cdot\nabla) u_s\rangle = 0$.
The rest of the proof proceeds identically to \cite{BaGa25}, up to replacing the application of Corollary 2.5 therein with \autoref{cor:flux_-1}.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
By \cref{thm_pathwise_uniq_euler} and \cref{thm_pathwise_uniq_order_0}, pathwise uniqueness for \eqref{eq:nonlinear_transport} holds in the class $\mathcal{E}$ for $\mathscr{R}=\mathscr{R}_{BS}$ or strictly subcritical regimes when $\mathscr{R}=\mathscr{R}_{CZ}$, and in $\mathcal{E}\cap \mathcal{U}_p$ in critical regimes for $\mathscr{R}=\mathscr{R}_{CZ}$.
    On the other hand, by \autoref{prop:convergence_law}, any limit points of the laws of $\{\theta^{\nu}\}_{\nu \in (0,1)}$ must be the law of a solution to \eqref{eq:nonlinear_transport} with values in $\mathcal{E}\cap \mathcal{U}_p$.
    Combining these facts with the classical Gyöngy--Krylov criterion \cite[Lemma 1.1]{gyongy1996existence} we can deduce that the whole family $\{\theta^\nu\}_{\nu \in (0,1)}$ converges, without the need to extract a subsequence, and that $\theta^\nu\to \theta$ in $\mathcal{E}$ in probability, without the need to pass to another probability space via Skorokhod's theorem.
    It follows that $\theta$ is adapted to the (augmented) filtration generated by $W$, since it is a limit in probability of adapted processes $\{\theta^\nu\}_{\nu \in (0,1)}$.
    Finally, Markovianity of $\theta$ follows by standard arguments, since we have established global strong existence and pathwise uniqueness for an SPDE with autonomous coefficients, whose driving noise $W$ has independent stationary increments.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- prop-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:girsanov}
    Let $\mathscr{R}=\mathscr{R}_{BS}$ and assume that \eqref{eq:parameters_anomalous_dissipation} holds; let the covariance of $W$ be given by $C_\alpha$ as in \eqref{eq:isotropic.covariance}.
    Let $\mu^{\theta}:=\mathcal{L}(\theta)$ and $\mu^{\rho}:=\mathcal{L}(\rho)$ be the laws of $\theta$ and $\rho$, solving respectively \eqref{eq:nonlinear_transport} and \eqref{eq:linear_kraichnan}, on $C([0,T];\mathcal{B}_{2})$.
    Then $\mu^{\theta}$ and $\mu^{\rho}$ are equivalent measures. Moreover, their relative entropies satisfy
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:relative_entropy_estimates}\begin{split}
        H(\mu^{\theta}|\mu^{\rho})&\leqslant \frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_0^T\|u_s\|_{\mathcal{H}_0}^2 \,\mathrm{d} s\right]<+\infty,\\
        H(\mu^{\rho}|\mu^{\theta})&\leqslant \frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_0^T\|\hat{u}_s\|_{\mathcal{H}_0}^2 \,\mathrm{d} s\right]<+\infty.
    \end{split}\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-6 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Let $a$ denote generic elements of $C_T \mathcal{B}_2$.
Fix $t\in[0,T]$ and let $\{s_n\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset [0,T]$ be such that $s_n\rightarrow t$ as $n\rightarrow+\infty$. Define the random variables
\begin{align*}
    X_n(a):=\|a(s_n)-a(t)\|_{L^2_x}^2.
\end{align*}
By \cite[Theorem 1.1]{DrGaPa25}, $X_n\rightarrow 0$ in measure with respect to $\mu^{\rho}$.
Since $\mu^\rho$ and $\mu^\theta$ are equivalent by \autoref{prop:girsanov}, it follows that $X_n\rightarrow 0$ in measure with respect to $\mu^{\theta}$ as well; namely,
\begin{align}\label{convergence_prob_theta}
    \|\theta_{s_n}-\theta_t\|_{L^2_x}^2\rightarrow 0\quad \mbox{in probability.}
\end{align}
On the other hand,
\begin{align*}
    \sup_{n\geqslant 1}\|\theta_{s_n}-\theta_t\|_{L^2_x}
    \leqslant 2 \sup_{t\in [0,T]} \|\theta_t\|_{L^2_x}
    \leqslant 2 \| \theta_0\|_{L^2_x}\quad \mathbb{P}\mbox{-a.s.}
\end{align*}
Therefore, \eqref{convergence_prob_theta} and dominated convergence imply the first claim in \eqref{eq:an_diss_euler}.

We now prove the second claim. 
Fix any $s<t$; by \cite[Proposition 2.6]{DrGaPa25}, $\rho$ is a Markov process, which combined with Remark 2.7 therein implies that
\begin{align*}
    \mathbb{P}(\| \rho_t\|_{L^2_x} \leqslant \| \rho_s\|_{L^2_x})=1.
\end{align*}
On the other hand, by \cite[Theorem 1.1]{DrGaPa25}, $\mathbb{E}[\rho_t\|_{L^2_x}]<\mathbb{E}[\rho_s\|_{L^2_x}]$, so that necessarily $\mathbb{P}(\| \rho_t\|_{L^2_x} < \| \rho_s\|_{L^2_x})>0$.
Since $\mu^\theta$ and $\mu^\rho$ are equivalent, this necessarily implies that
\begin{align*}
    \mathbb{P}(\| \theta_t\|_{L^2_x} \leqslant \| \theta_s\|_{L^2_x})=1, \quad \mathbb{P}(\| \theta_t\|_{L^2_x} < \| \theta_s\|_{L^2_x})>0
\end{align*}
and so we conclude that $\mathbb{E}[\| \theta_t\|_{L^2_x}]<\mathbb{E}[\| \theta_s\|_{L^2_x}]$.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- rmk-13: body_md -->
Consider the following family of viscous approximations of \eqref{eq:nonlinear_transport} with varying noise intensity (cf. \cite[p. 859]{le2002integration} and \cite[eq, (2.11)]{DrGaPa25} for some motivations)
\begin{align*}
    \begin{cases}
\,\mathrm{d} \bar\theta^\nu + \bar u^\nu \cdot \nabla \bar\theta^\nu \,\mathrm{d} t + \sqrt{1-\nu}\circ\, \mathrm{d} W \cdot \nabla \bar\theta^\nu =
   \frac{\nu}{2}\Delta \bar\theta^\nu \,\mathrm{d} t, \\
  \bar u^\nu := \mathscr{R}_{BS} \bar\theta^\nu,\\
  \bar\theta^\nu |_{t = 0} = \theta^\nu_0.
\end{cases}
\end{align*}
By readapting the Girsanov-type arguments presented above and exploiting the strong convergence in $L^2(\Omega\times \mathbb{R}^2)$ for the corresponding linear problems (see \cite[Lemma 2.8]{DrGaPa25}), one can easily verify that the following continuous-in-time anomalous dissipation property holds: for every $0\leqslant s<t\leqslant T$, 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:anomalous.girsanov}
    \lim_{\nu\rightarrow 0} \nu\int_s^t\mathbb{E}\left[\|\nabla\bar\theta^{\nu}_r\|_{L^2_x}^2\right]\,\mathrm{d} r>0.
\end{equation}
One can moreover redapt the arguments from \cite[Proposition 2.3]{DrGaPa25} to obtain equivalent characterizations of \eqref{eq:anomalous.girsanov}, e.g. at the level of the dissipation measure, and deduce a variant of \eqref{eq:anomalous.girsanov} with $\limsup_{\nu\to 0}$ inside the expectation rather than outside.
<!-- end rmk-13 -->
<!-- lem-8: body_md -->
\label{prop:dissipation.implies.irregularity}
Let $\alpha\in (0,1)$ and let $W$ be a noise whose covariance $C$ satisfies \eqref{eq:properties.C} and belongs to $C^{2\alpha}_x$. Let $\theta$ be a weak solution to the SPDE \eqref{eq:nonlinear_transport} with $f\equiv 0$ and $\theta\in L^\infty_{\omega,T} (L^r_x\cap L^p_x)$.
Suppose that $\mathscr{R},\alpha,r,p,\beta$ satisfy \autoref{ass:exponent.beta} with $\beta=1-\alpha$, namely that either of the following holds: 
\begin{itemize}
    \item $\mathscr{R} = \mathscr{R}_{BS}$, $r\in (1,2)$, $p\in [2,\infty)$ and $(\alpha,p)$ are such that $\beta=1-\alpha$ in \eqref{eq:definition.regularity};
    \item $\mathscr{R}=\mathscr{R}_{CZ}$, $r\in (1,2]$, $p\in [2,\infty)$ and $(\alpha,p)$ are such that $\beta=1-\alpha$ in \eqref{eq:definition.regularity_CZ}.
\end{itemize}
Let $0 \leqslant t_0 < t_1 \leqslant T$ and further assume that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:condition_no_anomalous1}
    \mathbb{P}\left( \theta\in E^{1-\alpha,2}_{[t_0,t_1]}\right)=1.
\end{equation}
Then 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:conclusion_no_anomalous1}
    \mathbb{P}\left( \|\theta_{t}\|_{L^2_x}=\|\theta_{t_0}\|_{L^2_x} \text{ for every } t\in [t_0,t_1]\right)=1.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lem-8 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Let $\chi\in C^\infty_c(\mathbb{R}^2)$ be a symmetric probability density supported in the annulus $B_1\setminus B_{1/2}$, and let $\{\chi^\varepsilon\}_{\varepsilon\in (0,1)}$ denote the associated family of standard mollifiers.

    
    Arguing as in \cite[Proposition 3.10]{DrGaPa25}, we can apply It\^o's formula to the process
    $\langle\theta^\varepsilon_t,\theta_t\rangle$ and use that $Q$ is divergence-free to obtain that $\mathbb{P}$-a.s.
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:approx_energy_balance}
        \langle\theta^\varepsilon_{t},\theta_{t}\rangle - \langle\theta^\varepsilon_{t_0},\theta_{t_0}\rangle
        = 2 \int_{t_0}^t \langle u_{s}\cdot\nabla \theta^\varepsilon_{s}, \theta_{s}\rangle \mathrm{d} {s}
        + 2 M^\varepsilon_{t} + \int_{t_0}^t \mathcal{A}^\varepsilon_{s} \mathrm{d} {s},\quad\forall t\in [t_0,t_1];
    \end{equation}
    in the above, the martingale $M^\varepsilon$ and the process $\mathcal{A}^\varepsilon$ are given by
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:no_anomalous_terms}
        M^\varepsilon_{t} =\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}} \int_{t_0}^{t} \langle \sigma_k\cdot\nabla\theta^\varepsilon_{s}, \theta_{s}\rangle \mathrm{d} W^k_{s}, \quad
        \mathcal{A}^\varepsilon_{s} = -\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^2} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} Q(\varepsilon z):D^2 \chi(z) \|\delta_{\varepsilon z}\theta_{s}\|_{L^2_x}^2 \mathrm{d} z,
    \end{equation}
    where we used the series representation \eqref{eq:noise_series_expansion}.
    Set $I^\varepsilon_s:= \langle u_{s}\cdot\nabla \theta^\varepsilon_{s}, \theta_{s}\rangle$; in view of \eqref{eq:approx_energy_balance} and properties of mollifiers, in order to deduce \eqref{eq:conclusion_no_anomalous1} it suffices to show that
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:no_anomalous_goal}
        \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0} \int_{t_0}^{t_1} |I^\varepsilon_s| \,\mathrm{d} s=0, \quad
        \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0} \int_{t_0}^{t_1} |\mathcal{A}^\varepsilon_{s}| \mathrm{d} {s}=0,\quad
        \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0} \sup_{t\in [t_0,t_1]} |M_t^\varepsilon|=0,
    \end{equation}
    where all limits above must be understood in probability.

    For notational convenience, throughout this proof let us adopt the notation
    \begin{align*}
        \left\llbracket\mkern-2.5mu\lbrack {\theta}\right\rbrack\mkern-2.5mu\rrbracket_\varepsilon:= \sup_{0<|z|\leqslant \varepsilon} \frac{1}{|z|^{1-\alpha}} \|\delta_z \theta\|_{L^2([t_0,t_1]\times \mathbb{R}^d)}
    \end{align*}
    so that assumption \eqref{eq:condition_no_anomalous1} amounts to the $\mathbb{P}$-a.s. convergence $\left\llbracket\mkern-2.5mu\lbrack {\theta}\right\rbrack\mkern-2.5mu\rrbracket_\varepsilon\to 0$ as $\varepsilon\to 0^+$ (see \eqref{eq:characterization_sharp_endpoint}).

    Concerning $\mathcal{A}^\varepsilon$, by \eqref{eq:no_anomalous_terms} and \eqref{eq:local.expansion.noise} we have
    \begin{align*}
        \limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0^+} \int_{t_0}^{t_1} |\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon}_{s}| \,\mathrm{d}{s}
        \lesssim \limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0^+} \frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2-2\alpha}} \int_{t_0}^{t_1} \int_{B_1}\|\delta_{\varepsilon z}\theta_s\|_{L^2_x}^2 \,\mathrm{d} z \,\mathrm{d} s
        \lesssim \left\llbracket\mkern-2.5mu\lbrack {\theta}\right\rbrack\mkern-2.5mu\rrbracket_\varepsilon^2
        \rightarrow 0.
    \end{align*}

    Since $M^\varepsilon$ are continuous martingales with $M^\varepsilon_{t_0}=0$, denoting by $[M^\varepsilon]$ their quadratic variations, in order to check \eqref{eq:no_anomalous_goal} for this term it suffices to show that $[M^\varepsilon]_{t_1}\to 0$ in probability as $\varepsilon\to 0^+$.

    A direct computation yields
    \begin{align*}
        [M^\varepsilon]_{t_1}
        & =\int_{t_0}^{t_1} \sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}} |\langle \sigma_k\cdot\nabla \theta^\varepsilon_{s}, \theta_{s}\rangle|^2 \mathrm{d} {s}\\
        & = \int_{t_0}^{t_1} \frac{1}{16\varepsilon^2} \sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}} \left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^2\times \mathbb{R}^2} \nabla\chi(z)\cdot \delta_{\varepsilon z} \sigma_k(x) |\delta_{\varepsilon z}\theta_{s}(x)|^2 \mathrm{d} x\mathrm{d} z\right|^2 \mathrm{d} {s}
        =: \frac{1}{16\varepsilon^2}\int_{t_0}^{t_1} J^\varepsilon_{s} \mathrm{d} {s},
    \end{align*}
    where we applied \autoref{lem:trilinear}. 
    By Minkowski's integral inequality,
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}} \| f_k\|_{L^1(\mathrm{d} x,\mathrm{d} z)}^2
        & =\Big\| \| f_k\|_{L^1(\mathrm{d} x,\mathrm{d} z)} \Big\|_{\ell^2(k)}^2\\
        & \leqslant \Big\| \|f_k(x,z)\|_{\ell^2(k)} \Big\|^2_{L^1(\mathrm{d} x,\mathrm{d} z)}
        = \Bigg[ \int_{\mathbb{R}^2\times \mathbb{R}^2} \bigg( \sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}} |f_k(x,z)|^2 \bigg)^{1/2} \mathrm{d} x \mathrm{d} z \Bigg]^2.
    \end{align*}
    Applying this to $J^\varepsilon_{s}$ yields
    \begin{align*}
        J^\varepsilon_{s}
        & \leqslant \Bigg[ \int_{\mathbb{R}^2\times \mathbb{R}^2} |\nabla\chi(z)| |\delta_{\varepsilon z}\theta_{s}(x)|^2  \bigg( \sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}} |\delta_{\varepsilon z}\sigma_k(x)|^2 \bigg)^{1/2} \mathrm{d} x \mathrm{d} z \Bigg]^2\\
        & = 2 \Bigg[ \int_{\mathbb{R}^2\times \mathbb{R}^2} |\nabla\chi(z)| |\delta_{\varepsilon z}\theta_{s}(x)|^2  \big( {\rm Tr} Q(\varepsilon z)\big)^{1/2} \mathrm{d} x \mathrm{d} z \Bigg]^2\\
        & = 2 \Bigg[ \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} |\nabla\chi(z)| \|\delta_{\varepsilon z}\theta_{s}\|_{L^2_x}^2  \big( {\rm Tr} Q(\varepsilon z)\big)^{1/2} \mathrm{d} z \Bigg]^2\\
        & \lesssim \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} |\nabla\chi(z)| \|\delta_{\varepsilon z}\theta_{s}\|_{L^2_x}^4  \, {\rm Tr} Q(\varepsilon z) \mathrm{d} z
    \end{align*}
    wehre in the second passage we used the identity \eqref{eq:covariance_series_expansion} and the definition of $Q$, while in the last step we applied Jensen's inequality, which is justified since $\nabla\chi\in L^1_x$.
    Using the assumption \eqref{eq:local.expansion.noise}, we infer that for all $\varepsilon>0$ sufficiently small
    \begin{align*}
        [M^\varepsilon]_{t_1}
        \lesssim \frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \int_{t_0}^{t_1} \int_{B_1} \|\delta_{\varepsilon z}\theta_{s}\|_{L^2_x}^4  \, {\rm Tr} Q(\varepsilon z) \mathrm{d} z
        \lesssim \frac{\varepsilon^{2(1-\alpha)} \varepsilon^{2\alpha} }{\varepsilon^2}\| \theta\|_{L^\infty_T L^2_x}^2 \left\llbracket\mkern-2.5mu\lbrack {\theta}\right\rbrack\mkern-2.5mu\rrbracket_\varepsilon^2
        \lesssim \left\llbracket\mkern-2.5mu\lbrack {\theta}\right\rbrack\mkern-2.5mu\rrbracket_\varepsilon^2\to 0
    \end{align*}
    which concludes the verification of \eqref{eq:no_anomalous_goal} for this term as well.

    It remains to treat $I^\varepsilon$. Note that this is the usual DiPerna--Lions commutator term, which by \autoref{lem:trilinear} satisfies
    \begin{align*}
        \int_{t_0}^{t_1} |I^\varepsilon_s| \,\mathrm{d} s
        & \lesssim \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \int_{t_0}^{t_1} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2\times \mathbb{R}^2}  |\nabla\chi(z)| |\delta_{\varepsilon z} u_s(x)| |\delta_{\varepsilon z}\theta_s(x)|^2 \,\mathrm{d} x \,\mathrm{d} z \,\mathrm{d} s\\
        & \lesssim \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \int_{t_0}^{t_1} \int_{B_1} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} |\delta_{\varepsilon z} u_s(x)| |\delta_{\varepsilon z}\theta_s(x)|^2 \,\mathrm{d} x \,\mathrm{d} z \,\mathrm{d} s;
    \end{align*}
    the last term above has almost the same structure as $\mathcal{T}_{u^\nu,\theta^\nu}(s,\varepsilon)$ previously estimated in \autoref{sec:computation}.
    In particular, obtaining uniform estimates for this term is very similar to verifying \eqref{eq:key_bound_regularization} with $\beta=1-\alpha$ and/or verifying \eqref{eq:anomalous_integrability_goal}. A couple observations are in order:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item[i)] Compared to the setting of the previous sections, we do not need to enforce any decomposition for either $u$ or $\theta$ in order to handle critical parameters: as soon as we obtain an estimate which contains a positive power of $\left\llbracket\mkern-2.5mu\lbrack {\theta}\right\rbrack\mkern-2.5mu\rrbracket_\varepsilon$, this term $\mathbb{P}$-a.s. converges to $0$ by assumption.
        \item[ii)] Applying \autoref{lem:final_besov} to deterministic functions, for any $\gamma\in (0,1)$, we similarly obtain the estimate
        \begin{align*}
            \int_{t_0}^{t_1} \| \delta_{\varepsilon z} \theta_s\|_{H^{\gamma(1-\alpha)}_x}^2 \,\mathrm{d} s \lesssim \varepsilon^{2(1-\gamma)(1-\alpha)} \left\llbracket\mkern-2.5mu\lbrack {\theta}\right\rbrack\mkern-2.5mu\rrbracket_\varepsilon^2, \quad\forall\varepsilon\in (0,1).
        \end{align*}
        \item[iii)] Many estimates performed in \autoref{sec:computation} are purely analytic, based on Sobolev embeddings and H\"older inequalities, and only use expectation in the end to make relevant norms like $\left\llbracket {\theta}\right\rrbracket_{\beta,\varrho_0}$ appear.
    \end{itemize}
    With these facts in mind, the verification that $\int_{t_0}^{t_1} |I^\varepsilon_s| \,\mathrm{d} s\to 0$ in probability now follows in the associated ranges of parameters by going through the same computations as in Cases 1, 4 and 5 from \autoref{sec:computation}.
    The only partial exception is the case $\mathscr{R}=\mathscr{R}_{BS}$ and $p\geqslant 3$; here we can use the more direct estimate
    \begin{align*}
        \int_{t_0}^{t_2} |I^\varepsilon_s| \,\mathrm{d} s
        & \leqslant \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \int_{B_1} \| \delta_{\varepsilon z} u\|_{L^3_{[t_0,t_1]} L^3_x} \| \delta_{\varepsilon z} \theta\|_{L^3_{[t_0,t_1]} L^3_x}^2 \,\mathrm{d} z\\
        & \lesssim \| \nabla u\|_{L^3_{[t_0,t_1]} L^3_x} \int_{B_1} \| \delta_{\varepsilon z} \theta\|_{L^3_{[t_0,t_1]} L^3_x}^2 \,\mathrm{d} z
        \lesssim \sup_{|z|\leqslant 1} \| \delta_{\varepsilon z} \theta\|_{L^3_{[t_0,t_1]} L^3_x}^2
    \end{align*}
    where the last quantity goes to $0$ $\mathbb{P}$-a.s. as $\varepsilon\to 0^+$ due to continuity of translations in $L^3_T L^3_x$.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- rmk-14: body_md -->
Going through similar computations, one can obtain the following variant of \autoref{prop:dissipation.implies.irregularity}: if condition \eqref{eq:condition_no_anomalous1} is replaced by
    \begin{align*}
        \lim_{|h|\to 0} \frac{1}{|h|^{2(1-\alpha)}} \int_{t_0}^{t_1} \mathbb{E}[\| \delta_h \theta_s\|_{L^2_x}^2] \mathrm{d} s=0,
    \end{align*}
    then instead of \eqref{eq:conclusion_no_anomalous1} one can conclude that $\mathbb{E}[\|\theta_{t}\|_{L^2}^2]=\mathbb{E}[\|\theta_{t_0}\|_{L^2}^2]$ for every $t\in [t_0,t_1]$.
<!-- end rmk-14 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
The proof of property \eqref{eq:an_diss_euler} was presented in \autoref{subsec:girsanov}.
    Concerning the last claim, if $\theta$ belonged to $L^2_\omega L^2_{[t_0,t_1]} B^{1-\alpha}_{2,q}$, then in particular by the facts recalled in \autoref{subsec:besov} one would have
    \begin{align*}
        \mathbb{P}\big(\theta \in E^{1-\alpha,2}_{[t_0,t_1]}\big)
        \geqslant \mathbb{P}\big(\theta \in L^2_{[t_0,t_1]} B^{1-\alpha}_{2,q}\big)=1
    \end{align*}
    which combined with \autoref{prop:dissipation.implies.irregularity} would contradict \eqref{eq:an_diss_euler}.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lem-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:flux_functions}
    Let $\alpha\in (0,1)$ and let $C$ satisfy \autoref{ass:noise2} with constants $c_1'$, $c_2'$. Let $s,\alpha\in (0,1)$ such that $s+\alpha\leqslant 1$ and consider
\begin{align*}
    & \dot F^n_s(\xi):= (2\pi)^{-d/2} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d}  \xi \cdot \hat{C}(\xi-\eta)\xi\, \Big( \frac{\varphi_n(|\eta|)}{|\eta|^{2s}}-\frac{\varphi_n(|\xi|)}{|\xi|^{2s}}\Big) \,\mathrm{d} \eta,\\
    & F^n_s(\xi):= (2\pi)^{-d/2} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \xi \cdot \hat{C}(\xi-\eta)\xi\, \Big( \frac{\varphi_n(|\eta|)}{\langle\eta\rangle^{2s}}-\frac{\varphi_n(|\xi|)}{\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}}\Big) \,\mathrm{d} \eta.
    \end{align*}
    Define similarly $F_s(\xi)$, $\dot F_s(\xi)$ by replacing $\varphi_n$ with the constant $1$ in the previous lines.
    Then there exists a constant $K=K(d,s,\alpha,c_1',c_2')$ such that
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pointwise_bound_approx}
        |F_s(\xi)| + |\dot F_s(\xi)| + \sup_n |\dot F^n_s(\xi)| +  \sup_n | F^n_s(\xi)|\leqslant K \langle \xi\rangle^{2(1-\alpha-s)} \quad \forall \xi\in\mathbb{R}^d.
    \end{equation}
    Moreover, there exist constants $K_1,K_2>0$, depending on the same parameters as $K$, such that
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:good_bound}
        F_s(\xi) \leqslant -K_1 \langle \xi\rangle^{2(1-\alpha-s)} + K_2 \langle \xi\rangle^{-2s}\quad \forall \xi\in\mathbb{R}^d.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end lem-9 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
By \autoref{ass:noise2}, one has $\hat C=c_1' \hat C_\alpha + \hat R$ with $\hat C_\alpha$ given by \eqref{eq:isotropic.covariance}. We divide the proof in two steps.

    \textit{Step 1.} First assume $c_2'=0$, namely $R\equiv 0$. By homogeneity we can further assume $c_1'=0$ and $C=C_\alpha$; $\dot F_s^n$ is then given by
    \begin{equation*}
        \dot F^n_s(\xi):= (2\pi)^{-d/2} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \frac{1}{\langle \xi-\eta\rangle^{d+2\alpha}} |P^\perp_{\xi-\eta}\xi|^2 \Big( \frac{\varphi_n(|\eta|)}{|\eta|^{2s}}-\frac{\varphi_n(|\xi|)}{|\xi|^{2s}}\Big) \,\mathrm{d} \eta,
    \end{equation*}
    similarly for $F_s^n$.

    For $|\xi|\leqslant 1$, estimate \eqref{eq:pointwise_bound_approx} follows from the bound $|P^\perp_{\xi-\eta}\xi|^2\leqslant |\xi|^2 \leqslant 1$, that fact that $|\eta|^{-2s}, \langle \eta\rangle^{-2s}\in L^1_x+L^\infty_x$ and Young's convolution inequalities, see the proof of \cite[Proposition 3.2]{GaGrMa24}.

    Assume now $|\xi|\geqslant 1$. Following \cite[Proposition 3.2]{GaGrMa24}, for given $\xi$, one can define
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:domains}\begin{split}
         & D_1:=\{\eta\in \mathbb{R}^d: |\eta-\xi|\wedge |\eta|>|\xi|/2\},\\
         & D_2:=\{\eta\in \mathbb{R}^d: |\eta-\xi|> |\xi|/2, \ |\eta|\leqslant |\xi|/2\},\\
         & D_3:=\{\eta\in \mathbb{R}^d: |\eta-\xi|\leqslant |\xi|/2\}.
    \end{split}\end{equation}
    Accordingly we can write $\dot F^n_s=\sum_{i=1}^3 \dot F^n_{s,i}$, where $\dot F^n_{s,i}$ is defined as $\dot F^n_s$ but with the integral taken over $D_i$.   
    The term $\dot F^n_3$ can be estimated identically as in \cite{GaGrMa24} and is thus omitted (see also Step 2 below).
    Noting that $|\eta-\xi|\sim |\eta|$ on $D_1$, we can estimate $\dot F^n_{s,1}$ by
    \begin{align*}
        |\dot F^n_{s,1}(\xi)|
        \lesssim |\xi|^2 \int_{|\eta|>|\xi|/2}  \frac{1}{|\eta|^{d+2\alpha+2s}} \,\mathrm{d} \eta + |\xi|^{2-2s} \int_{|\eta|>|\xi|/2}  \frac{1}{|\eta|^{d+2\alpha}} \,\mathrm{d} \eta
        \lesssim |\xi|^{2-2\alpha-2s}.
    \end{align*}
    On $D_2$ it holds $|\xi|\sim |\eta-\xi|$, therefore
    \begin{align*}
        |\dot F^n_{s,2}(\xi)|
        & \leqslant \int_{D_2} \frac{1}{\langle \xi-\eta\rangle^{d+2\alpha}} \Big( \frac{
        |\xi|^2}{|\eta|^{2s}}+|\xi|^{2-2s}\Big) \,\mathrm{d} \eta\\
        & \lesssim |\xi|^{2-2\alpha-d} \int_{|\eta|\leqslant |\xi|/2 } \frac{
        1}{|\eta|^{2s}} \,\mathrm{d} \eta + |\xi|^{2-2s} \int_{|\xi-\eta|\geqslant |\xi|/2 } \frac{1}{\langle \xi-\eta\rangle^{d+2\alpha}} \,\mathrm{d} \eta
        \lesssim |\xi|^{2-2\alpha-2s}.
    \end{align*}
    Combining the above estimates gives the $n$-uniform bound for $|\dot F^n_s(\xi)|$.

    Concerning $|F^n_s(\xi)|$, whenever $|\xi|\geqslant R=R(\alpha,s)\gg 1$, by \cite[Lemma A.2]{crippa2025zero} it holds
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:estimate_CrLuPa}
        |\dot F_s(\xi)-F_s(\xi)| + \sup_{n} |\dot F^n_s(\xi)-F^n_s(\xi)| \leqslant C' \langle \xi\rangle^{-2s (\frac{d+2\alpha}{d+2})},
    \end{equation}
    which combined with the bound for $\sup_n |\dot F^n_s(\xi)|$ allows to conclude. Instead for $|\xi|\leqslant R$, we have the trivial estimate
    \begin{equation*}
        |F^n_s(\xi)| \lesssim |\xi|^2 \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \frac{1}{\langle \xi-\eta\rangle^{d+2\alpha}} \,\mathrm{d} \eta \lesssim |\xi|^2 \leqslant R^{2(\alpha+s)} |\xi|^{2(1-\alpha-s)}.
    \end{equation*}
    Having established uniform-in-$n$ bounds for $\dot F^n_s$, $F^n_s$, the pointwise estimates for $\dot F_s$, $F_s$ follow by taking $n\to\infty$, concluding the proof of \eqref{eq:pointwise_bound_approx}.

    It remains to show \eqref{eq:good_bound}.
    By \cite[Proposition 4.2]{GaGrMa24}, there exist constants $\tilde K_1$, $\tilde K_2$ such that
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:estimate_GaGrMa}
        \dot F_s(\xi) \leqslant - \tilde K_1 |\xi|^{2(1-\alpha-s)}+\tilde K_2 |\xi|^{-2s} \quad \forall \xi\in \mathbb{R}^d\setminus\{0\}.
    \end{equation}
    Together with \eqref{eq:estimate_CrLuPa} and Young's inequality, for $|\xi|\geqslant R$ this implies the existence of constants $C''$, $\tilde K_3>0$ such that
    \begin{align*}
        F_s(\xi)
        &\leqslant - \tilde K_1 |\xi|^{2(1-\alpha-s)} + \tilde K_2 |\xi|^{-2s} +  C' \langle \xi\rangle^{-2s (\frac{d+2\alpha}{d+2})}\\
        & \leqslant -\frac{\tilde K_1}{2} |\xi|^{2(1-\alpha-s)} + (\tilde K_2+C'') |\xi|^{-2s}
        \leqslant -\frac{\tilde K_1}{4} \langle \xi\rangle^{2(1-\alpha-s)} + \tilde K_3 \langle \xi\rangle^{-2s}
    \end{align*}
    where we used that $|\xi|\geqslant R\gg 1$ and $\frac{d+2\alpha}{d+2}<1$.
    On the other hand, for $|\xi|\leqslant R$, by virtue of \eqref{eq:pointwise_bound_approx} it holds
    \begin{align*}
        |F_s(\xi)|\leqslant C\langle \xi\rangle^{2(1-\alpha-s)} \pm \frac{\tilde K_1}{4} \langle \xi\rangle^{2(1-\alpha-s)} \leqslant - \frac{\tilde K_1}{4} \langle \xi\rangle^{2(1-\alpha-s)} + \Big(C+\frac{\tilde K_1} {4}\Big) \langle R\rangle^{2(1-\alpha)} \langle \xi\rangle ^{-2s}.
    \end{align*}
    Up to relabelling constants, this yields \eqref{eq:good_bound}.
    
    \textit{Step 2.} Consider now $c_2'\neq 0$. To conclude, it suffices to show that the contributions associated to $\hat R$ can be absorbed in the r.h.s. of \eqref{eq:pointwise_bound_approx}. Namely, letting $H_s$, $\dot{H}_s$, $\dot H^n_s$, $H^n_s$ denote the analogues of $F_s$, $\dot F_s$, $\dot F^n_s$, $F^n_s$ with $\hat C$ replaced by $\hat R$, we want to show that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pointwise_bound_step2}
        |H_s(\xi)| + |\dot H_s(\xi)| + \sup_n |\dot H^n_s(\xi)| +  \sup_n | H^n_s(\xi)|\leqslant c_2' \langle \xi\rangle^{-2s} \quad \forall \xi\in\mathbb{R}^d.
    \end{equation}
    The proof of \eqref{eq:pointwise_bound_step2} holds for any $s\in (0,1]$, independently of $\alpha$.
    We show \eqref{eq:pointwise_bound_step2} only for $\dot H^n_s$ and $|\xi|\geqslant 1$; the other cases are similar and left to the reader.
    Let $D_i$ be defined as in \eqref{eq:domains} and consider $\dot H^n_{s,i}$ as before. On $D_1$, since $|\eta-\xi|\sim |\eta| > |\xi|/2$, we have
    \begin{align*}
        |\dot H^n_{s,1}(\xi)|
        \lesssim \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} |\xi|^2 |\hat R(\xi-\eta)| \left(\frac{1}{|\eta|^{2s}}+\frac{1}{|\xi|^{2s}}\right) \mathrm{d}\eta
        \lesssim \frac{1}{|\xi|^{2s}} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} |\xi-\eta|^2 |\hat R(\xi-\eta)| \mathrm{d} \eta
        \lesssim c_2' \langle \xi\rangle^{-2s}.
    \end{align*}
    On $D_2$, thanks to $|\eta-\xi|\sim |\xi|\geqslant 2|\eta|$ and the cancellation property \eqref{eq:cancellation_fourier_covariance}, it holds
    \begin{align*}
        |\dot H^n_{s,2}(\xi)|
        & \lesssim \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} |\eta|^{2-2s} |\hat R(\xi-\eta)| \mathrm{d} \eta
        \lesssim \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} |\xi|^{2-2s} |\hat R(\xi-\eta)| \mathrm{d} \eta\\
        & \lesssim \langle \xi\rangle^{-2s} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} |\xi-\eta|^2 |\hat R(\xi-\eta)| \mathrm{d} \eta
        \leqslant c_2' \langle \xi\rangle^{-2s} 
    \end{align*}
    where we used that $s\leqslant 1$.
    Finally, on $D_3$ we need to go through the same argument as \cite[Proposition 3.2, Step 2]{GaGrMa24}. Letting $\psi_n$ be defined as therein, using the fact that
    \begin{align*}
        (\eta-\xi)\mapsto [\xi\cdot \hat R(\xi-\eta) \xi]\,\nabla\psi_n(\xi)\cdot(\eta-\xi)
    \end{align*}
    is an odd function (for fixed $\xi$) due to the evenness of $\hat R$, one ends up finding once again the estimate
    \begin{align*}
        |\dot H^n_{s,3}|
        \lesssim \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} |\xi\cdot \hat R(\xi-\eta)\xi| \, |\xi-\eta|^2 \, \frac{1}{|\xi|^{2+2s}} \mathrm{d} \eta
        \lesssim c_2'\langle \xi\rangle^{-2s}.
    \end{align*}
    Overall this concludes the verification of \eqref{eq:pointwise_bound_step2} and the proof.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- cor-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:flux_-1}
    Let $d=2$, $\alpha\in (0,1)$ and let $C$ satisfy \autoref{ass:noise2} with constants $c_1'$, $c_2'$. Set $Q(z)=C(0)-C(z)$ and let $\dot F_1$ be defined as in \autoref{lem:flux_functions} for $s=1$.
    Let $G$ denote the Green function associated to $(-\Delta)^{-1}$.
    Then there exist constants $K_1,K_2>0$, depending on the parameters $\alpha,c_1',c_2'$ such that
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:good_bound_-1}
        \mathrm{Tr} [\widehat{Q\,D^2 G}](\xi)  = \dot F_1(\xi) \leqslant -K_1 | \xi|^{-2\alpha} + K_2 |\xi|^{-2}, \quad \forall \xi\in\mathbb{R}^d.
    \end{equation}
    Moreover one has $|F_1(\xi)|\lesssim 1$ uniformly in $\xi\in\mathbb{R}^d$.
<!-- end cor-3 -->
<!-- lem-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:auxiliary.besov.1}
    For any $\beta \in (0,1)$, $\varrho_0\in (0,\infty)$, $T_0 \in [0,T]$ and for every $h \in \mathbb{R}^d$ with $|h| \in (0,\varrho_0)$ it holds $\mathbb{P}$-a.s. 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:restricted_increments}
        \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \| \delta_h f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2 \right] \lesssim_d |h|^{2\beta} \left\llbracket {f  }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,\varrho_0 \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^2,
    \end{equation}
    where the hidden constant does not depend on $h,\varrho_0,T_0,T$.
<!-- end lem-10 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
We divide the proof in several passages.
    
    \emph{Step 1.} Let $\psi_{B_{\varrho}}:=|B_{\varrho}|^{-1} \bm{1}_{B_{\varrho}}$ denote the characteristic function of the closed unit ball of radius ${\varrho}>0$, renormalized to be a probability density. We claim that, for any ${\varrho} < {\varrho}_0$, one has the a.s. bound
    \begin{equation} \label{eq:localized_ball_estim}
      \mathbb{E}_{T_0}  \left[ \| f - f\ast\psi_{B_{\varrho}}\|^2_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}} \right] 
        \leqslant 
        \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[
        \left(
        \fint_{B_{\varrho}} \| \delta_h f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}} \,\mathrm{d} h
        \right)^2 \right]
        \lesssim 
        \varrho^{2\beta} \left\llbracket {f }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,{\varrho}_0 \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^2.
    \end{equation}
    The first inequality is an immediate consequence of definitions and Minkowski's integral inequality.
    Concerning the second one, by Jensen's inequality and Cavalieri's principle, it holds $\mathbb{P}$-a.s.
    \begin{align*}
        \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \left( \fint_{B_{\varrho}} \| \delta_h f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}} \,\mathrm{d} h \right)^2  \right]
        & \lesssim 
        \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ 
        \frac{1}{{\varrho}^d} \int_{|h|\leqslant {\varrho}} \| \delta_h f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2 \,\mathrm{d} h  \right]
        \\
        & \lesssim 
        \frac{1}{{\varrho}^d} \int_0^{\varrho} r^{d-1+2\beta}
        \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ r^{-2\beta}
        \int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}  \| \delta_{r z} f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2  \sigma(\mathrm{d} z) \right]
        \mathrm{d} r
        \\
        & \lesssim
        \varrho^{2\beta}  \left\llbracket {f  }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,{\varrho}_0 \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^2 .
    \end{align*}
    
    \emph{Step 2.} 
    In what follows we will frequently denote translation operators by $\tau_h$, namely $\tau_h f:= f(h+\cdot)$.
    Here we prove the bound \eqref{eq:restricted_increments} for $h\in \mathbb{R}^d$ with $|h|={\varrho} < {\varrho}_0/3$.
    Rewrite
    \begin{align*}
        \delta_h f
        =
        \left( \tau_h f - \tau_h f\ast \psi_{B_{\varrho}}\right) 
        + 
        \left( \tau_h f\ast \psi_{B_{\varrho}} - f\ast \psi_{B_{\varrho}} \right) 
        + 
        \left( f\ast \psi_{B_{\varrho}} - f \right). 
    \end{align*}
    Observe that the translation $\tau_h$ and the convolution with $\psi_{B_{\varrho}}$ commute, so no ambiguity arises in the quantity $\tau_h f\ast \psi_{B_{\varrho}}$ above.
    By triangular inequality and applying \eqref{eq:localized_ball_estim} twice we get
    \begin{align*}
        \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \| \delta_h f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2  \right]
        & \lesssim
        \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \| \tau_h f - \tau_h f\ast \psi_{B_{\varrho}}\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2 \right]
        \\
        &\quad+ 
        \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \| \tau_h f\ast \psi_{B_{\varrho}} - f\ast \psi_{B_{\varrho}}\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2 \right] 
        \\
        &\quad+ 
        \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \| f\ast \psi_{B_{\varrho}} - f \|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2 \right] 
        \\
        & \lesssim |h|^{2\beta} \left\llbracket {f }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,{\varrho}_0 \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^2 
        + 
        \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \| \tau_h f\ast \psi_{B_{\varrho}} - f\ast \psi_{B_{\varrho}}\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2  \right].
    \end{align*}
    The first term in the right-hand side above is the same appearing in \eqref{eq:restricted_increments}, thus we only need to bound the second term.
    On the other hand, by Minkowski's integral inequality, we have $\mathbb{P}$-almost surely
    \begin{align*}
      \mathbb{E}_{T_0} &\left[ \| \tau_h f\ast \psi_{B_{\varrho}} - f\ast \psi_{B_{\varrho}}\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2   \right]
        \\
        &\leqslant 
         \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \left(
         \fint_{B_{\varrho}} \fint_{B_{\varrho}} \| f(h+z+\cdot)-f(\tilde z+\cdot)\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}} \,\mathrm{d} z \,\mathrm{d} \tilde z \right)^2   \right]
         \\
         & \lesssim 
         \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \left(
         \fint_{B_{\varrho}} \fint_{B_{3{\varrho}}} \| f(y+\tilde z +\cdot)-f(\tilde z+\cdot)\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}} \,\mathrm{d} y \,\mathrm{d} \tilde z
         \right)^2  \right]
         \\
         & = 
         \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \left(
         \fint_{B_{3{\varrho}}} \| \delta_y f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}} \,\mathrm{d} y
         \right)^2  \right]
         \lesssim 
        \varrho^{2\beta} \left\llbracket {f }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,{\varrho}_0 \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^2
        =
        |h|^{2\beta} \left\llbracket {f }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,{\varrho}_0 \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^2,
    \end{align*}
    where we used that $y:= z+h-\tilde z$ satisfies $|y|\leqslant 3{\varrho} < {\varrho}_0$ in the intermediate passage, while in the last line we invoked \eqref{eq:localized_ball_estim}.

    \emph{Step 3.} For $|h|={\varrho}\in [{\varrho}_0/3,{\varrho}_0)$, we can just write
    \begin{align*}
        \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \| \delta_h f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2  \right]
        &= 
        \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[
        \|\tau_{2h/3} (\delta_{h/3} f) + \tau_{h/3} (\delta_{h/3} f)  + \delta_{h/3}f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2  \right]
        \\
        &\lesssim 
        \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \| \delta_{h/3} f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2  \right]
        \lesssim 
        |h|^{2\beta} \left\llbracket {f }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,{\varrho}_0 \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^2,
    \end{align*}
    where the last estimate follows from the previous step, concluding the verification of \eqref{eq:restricted_increments}.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- lem-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:final_besov}
    Fix $\beta,\varrho_0,T_0$ as in \cref{lem:auxiliary.besov.1}.
    For any $\gamma\in [0,1)$ and any $h\in \mathbb{R}^d$ with $|h| < {\varrho}_0 \leqslant 1$, one has the $\mathbb{P}$-a.s. bounds
    \begin{align} 
        & \int_{T_0}^T \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[\| \delta_h f_t\|_{H^{\beta\gamma}_x}^2  \right] \,\mathrm{d} t \lesssim
        |h|^{2(1-\gamma)\beta} \left\llbracket { f }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,{\varrho}_0 \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^2,\label{eq:final.besov1}\\
        & \int_{T_0}^T \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[\| f_t\|_{\dot H^{\beta\gamma}_x}^2  \right] \,\mathrm{d} t 
        \lesssim \mathbb{E}_{T_0}\left[\| f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^{2}\right]^{1-\gamma} \left\llbracket { f    }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,+\infty \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^{2\gamma}. \label{eq:final.besov2}
    \end{align}
    where the hidden constants do not depend on $h,{\varrho}_0,T_0,T$.
<!-- end lem-11 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Denote by $\{\Delta_j\}_{j \geqslant -1}$ the inhomogeneous Littlewood--Paley blocks in the space variable;
    arguing as in \cite[Lemma 2.18]{DrGaPa25} (up to taking conditional expectation instead of plain expectation in the proof thereof) it holds $\mathbb{P}$-a.s.
    \begin{equation} \label{eq:LP_bound.aux}
        \sup_{j> -1} 2^{2\beta j} \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \| \Delta_j \delta_h f\|^2_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}
          \right]
        \lesssim  
        \sup_{\varepsilon>0} \frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2\beta}} \fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}} 
        \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \|\delta_{\varepsilon z} (\delta_h f)\|^2_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}   \right]\sigma(\mathrm{d} z)
        .
    \end{equation}
As in the case of $\sup_{\varrho \in (0,\varrho_0)}G_{\beta,T_0}(\varrho,f)$, the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:LP_bound.aux} is a well-defined $\mathcal{F}_{T_0}$-measurable random variable.

We focus on \eqref{eq:final.besov1}. Let us distinguish two cases: if $\varepsilon \leqslant  |h|$, since $\delta_{\varepsilon z}$ and $\delta_h$ commute, by \cref{lem:auxiliary.besov.1} we find
\begin{align*}
    \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[
    \|\delta_{\varepsilon z} (\delta_h f)\|^2_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}
     \right] 
    &=
    \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[
    \|\delta_h (\delta_{\varepsilon z} f)\|^2_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}
      \right]
    \\
    &\lesssim
    \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[
    \| \delta_{\varepsilon z} f \|^2_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}
      \right]
    \lesssim
    \varepsilon^{2\beta}  \left\llbracket {f  }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,\varrho_0 \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^2;
\end{align*}
Instead, if $\varepsilon > |h|$, one can use the trivial bound
\begin{align*}
   \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[
    \|\delta_{\varepsilon z} (\delta_h f)\|^2_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}
      \right]
    \lesssim
    \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[
    \| \delta_h f\|^2_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}
      \right]
    \lesssim
    \varepsilon^{2\beta}  \left\llbracket {f  }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,\varrho_0 \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^2.
\end{align*}
In either case, from \eqref{eq:LP_bound.aux} and the above estimates we deduce
\begin{align}\label{eq:LP_bound}
    \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \| \Delta_j \delta_h f\|^2_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}
         \right]
        \lesssim 
        2^{-2\beta j} \left\llbracket {f  }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,\varrho_0 \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^2.
\end{align}
By the Besov identifications $H^{\beta \gamma}_x= B^{\beta\gamma}_{2,2}$ and $L^2_x=B^{0}_{2,2}$, we have for any $N \in \mathbb{N}$
\begin{align*}
    \int_0^T \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \| \delta_h f_t\|_{H^{\beta\gamma}_x}^2   \right] \,\mathrm{d} t
        &\sim 
        \sum_{j\geqslant -1} 2^{2j\beta\gamma} \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[  \| \Delta_j \delta_h f_t\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2   \right]
        \\
        & \lesssim 
        2^{2N\beta\gamma} \sum_{-1\leqslant j\leqslant N} \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[  \| \Delta_j \delta_h f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2   \right]
        + 
        \sum_{j> N} 2^{-2 j\beta (1-\gamma)}
        \left\llbracket {f  }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,\varrho_0 \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^2
        \\
        & \lesssim 
        2^{2N\beta\gamma} \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[  \| \delta_h f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2  \right]
        + 
        2^{-2 N \beta  (1-\gamma) } \left\llbracket {f  }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,\varrho_0 \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^2
        \\
        & \lesssim 
        \left( 2^{2N\beta\gamma} |h|^{2\beta} + 2^{-2N\beta (1-\gamma)} \right) \left\llbracket {f  }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,\varrho_0 \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^2,
    \end{align*}
    where in the above passages we used \eqref{eq:LP_bound} and \cref{lem:auxiliary.besov.1}. Choosing $N$ such that $2^{-N}\sim |h|$ then yields the desired \eqref{eq:final.besov1}.

    The proof of \eqref{eq:final.besov2} is similar, using the homogeneous Littlewood--Paley blocks $\{\dot\Delta_j\}_{j \in\mathbb{Z}}$, the identification $\dot H^{\beta\gamma}_x=\dot B^{\beta\gamma}_{2,2}$ and similar optimization arguments with $N\in\mathbb{Z}$; the only difference is the use of the Bernstein estimate $\| \dot\Delta_j f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2 \lesssim 2^{2j} \| f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2$ valid for all $j\in\mathbb{Z}$.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- cor-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:besov.higher.time}
Under the same assumptions of \cref{lem:final_besov}, for every $t_\ast \in [0,T-T_0]$ it holds
    \begin{align*}   
        & \int_{T_0}^{T_0+t_\ast} \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \| f_t\|_{H^{\beta\gamma}_x}^2   \right] \,\mathrm{d} t\\
        & \ \ \lesssim (1+\varrho_0^{-2\beta\gamma})\mathbb{E}_{T_0}\left[\| f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^{2}\right] + \mathbb{E}_{T_0}\left[\| f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^{2}\right]^{1-\gamma} \left\llbracket { f  \mathbf{1}_{[T_0, T_0+t_\ast]}}\right\rrbracket_{\beta,+\infty \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^{2\gamma}\\
        & \ \ \lesssim t_\ast (1+\varrho_0^{-2\beta\gamma}) \left\| \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \| f \|_{L^2_x}^2    \right] \right\|_{L^\infty_{[T_0,T_0+t_\ast]}}
        + t_\ast^{1-\gamma} \left\| \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \| f \|_{L^2_x}^2    \right] \right\|_{L^\infty_{[T_0,T_0+t_\ast]}}^{1-\gamma} \left\llbracket { f  \mathbf{1}_{[T_0, T_0+t_\ast]}}\right\rrbracket_{\beta,+\infty \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^{2\gamma}.
    \end{align*} 
    where the hidden constant does not depend on ${\varrho}_0,T_0,t_\ast,T$.
<!-- end cor-4 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Without loss of generality we may assume $t_\ast=T-T_0$. The first estimate follows from combining \eqref{eq:final.besov2} with the bounds
    \begin{align*}
        \| f_t\|_{H^{\beta\gamma}_x}^2 \lesssim \| f_t\|_{L^2_x}^2 + \| f_t\|_{\dot H^{\beta\gamma}_x}^2,\quad
        \left\llbracket { f    }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,+\infty \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^2 \lesssim \varrho_0^{-2\beta} \mathbb{E}_{T_0}\left[ \| f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^2\right]+\left\llbracket { f    }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,\varrho_0 \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}^2
    \end{align*}
    where the second one follows from the definition of $\left\llbracket { f    }\right\rrbracket_{\beta,+\infty \mid \mathcal{F}_{T_0}}$ and triangular inequality.
    The second estimate then follows from the first one combined with
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}_{T_0}\left[\| f\|_{L^2_{[T_0,T],x}}^{2}\right] \leqslant t_\ast \left\| \mathbb{E}_{T_0} \left[ \| f \|_{L^2_x}^2    \right] \right\|_{L^\infty_{[T_0,T]}}. \qedhere
    \end{equation*}
<!-- end proof-22 -->
